1. **GPU status**: 
   - GPU 0: 0% util, 49175 MiB used (model loaded but idle? Or residual memory)

2. **Training log**: 
   - Currently at step 178/402 (~44%), running at ~10-11s/it
   - Remaining: 224 steps × ~11s = ~41 minutes
   - This is the MBPP→HE training (batch script running both mbpp2he conditions sequentially)
   
There's a mismatch between the GPU assignments in the config and what's actually running — the batch script is pinned to cuda:1 and that's where all the activity is, but the individual experiment commands specify --gpu 0, which appears idle. The batch script is running on cuda:1 with individual experiments specifying --gpu 0, which maps to physical GPU 1 when CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=1 is set—that's the expected behavior. The 49GB of unused memory on GPU 0 is likely leftover from a previous experiment that wasn't properly freed, so I should verify the project's GPU allocation constraints to confirm there's no violation.

Looking at the experimental results, the cross-dataset HE→MBPP transfer is striking: exception_only achieves 99.41% accuracy while no_trace only reaches 70.56%, barely above the majority baseline of 69.02%—this strongly supports the claim that exception events contain transferable signal. Now I need to verify the HE→MBPP completed experiments more carefully and check on the paper revision status.

### Assistant

_source line 1479 · 2026-05-22T06:15:43.614Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Let me verify the data setup:
- HE→MBPP train: 1640 samples (HumanEval-based training, 164 problems × 10 samples)
- HE→MBPP test: 4270 samples (MBPP test set)
- no_trace test: 4270 samples (same MBPP test set, no trace condition)

This confirms the cross-dataset setup: trained on HumanEval (164 problems), tested on MBPP (427 problems). The test set is much larger than the training set.

Key results to verify:


The exception_only model achieves 99.41% accuracy on MBPP despite training on just 1640 HumanEval samples, which is striking evidence that exception patterns transfer across datasets. In contrast, the no_trace variant only reaches 70.56%, barely edging out the 69.02% majority baseline by 1.55 percentage points.

This gap is telling — without exception traces, the model fails to capture anything meaningful that generalizes from HumanEval to MBPP. Before finalizing these results, I should verify the GPU resource allocation to make sure everything ran under consistent conditions.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Let me verify against registry:

**cross_he2mbpp_exception_only_seed42**:
- Registry: accuracy=0.9941, pass_precision=0.998, pass_recall=0.9936, pass_f1=0.9957, fail_precision=0.9858, fail_recall=0.9955, fail_f1=0.9906, majority_baseline=0.6902, lift=0.304
- Eval JSON: accuracy=0.9941..., pass_precision=0.9979..., pass_recall=0.9935..., pass_f1=0.9957..., fail_precision=0.9857..., fail_recall=0.9954..., fail_f1=0.9905..., majority_baseline=0.6901..., lift=0.3039...


- ✅ Registry matches (rounded to 4 decimal places)

**cross_he2mbpp_no_trace_seed42**:
- Registry: accuracy=0.7056, pass_precision=0.736, pass_recall=0.8941, pass_f1=0.8074, fail_precision=0.5478, fail_recall=0.2857, fail_f1=0.3756, majority_baseline=0.6902, lift=0.0155
- Eval JSON: accuracy=0.7056..., pass_precision=0.7360..., pass_recall=0.8941..., pass_f1=0.8074..., fail_precision=0.5478..., fail_recall=0.2857..., fail_f1=0.37555..., majority_baseline=0.6901..., lift=0.0155...
- ✅ Registry matches (rounded to 4 decimal places)

The exception_only variant shows remarkable cross-dataset transfer at 99.41%, actually exceeding the in-distribution VOTA performance, while no_trace barely clears the majority baseline at 70.56% with minimal lift. The 28.85 percentage point gap between these two approaches is slightly wider than the in-distribution gap, and the no_trace model's failure recall is critically low at just 28%.

This strongly supports the core finding that exception events encode transferable diagnostic information — without execution traces, the model essentially defaults to predicting "pass" on unfamiliar datasets. I'm considering potential confounds: data leakage between HE and MBPP seems unlikely given the 99.41% success rate across 4270 samples is nearly identical to in-distribution performance, and while the single seed (42) without error bars is a limitation, the magnitude of the difference (99.41% vs 70.56%) is too stark to be noise.

The analysis is limited to Qwen-7B though, so cross-model validation would strengthen the claim. Looking at the MBPP baseline of 69.02% pass rate, the no_trace model's 28.57% fail recall means it's actually catching fewer failures than the base rate — it's essentially guessing "pass" most of the time. Now I should check whether the paper has been revised since my last review.
```

```text
1	\documentclass[11pt,a4paper]{article}
2	\usepackage[review]{acl}
3	% \usepackage{acl}  % uncomment for camera-ready (remove [review])
4	\usepackage{times,latexsym}
5	\usepackage{amsmath,amssymb}
6	\usepackage{graphicx}
7	\graphicspath{{../../figures/paper/}}
8	\usepackage{booktabs}
9	\usepackage{multirow}
10	\usepackage{xcolor}
11	\usepackage{algorithm}
12	\usepackage{algorithmic}
13	
14	\title{Complete Execution Traces Degrade SFT-Based Verdict Prediction: \\A Diagnostic Signal Analysis}
15	\author{Anonymous ACL submission}
16	
17	\begin{document}
18	\maketitle
19	
20	\begin{abstract}
21	Complete execution traces contain all information needed for verdict prediction (classifying whether a test passes or fails), yet they actively impair SFT learning.
22	On LLM-generated Python code, across four models spanning three architecture families (1.5B--13B), complete traces achieve only 77--82\% accuracy despite containing strictly more diagnostic information than compact exception events (${\geq}$99.33\%), an 18+pp degradation from \emph{more} input.
23	Training dynamics reveal the mechanism: verbose traces cause models to overfit to spurious patterns. \texttt{full\_trace} with label-only loss (gradient dilution reduced) oscillates between 82--89\% across all training steps without stable convergence, while \texttt{exception\_only} converges within 50 steps.
24	A preliminary decomposition on the Qwen family (two scales, three seeds each) suggests gradient dilution accounts for at most ${\sim}$26\% of the gap, with the remainder attributable to information overload at inference, though label-only loss simultaneously alters multiple training dynamics.
25	A sharp performance jump at ${\sim}$500 tail tokens across all four models marks where tail truncation crosses the globals module dump threshold, exposing structured execution markers (\texttt{[Line~X]} variable values).
26	A two-tier signal hierarchy holds cross-family: Tier~1 (exceptions, assertions; ${\geq}$98.53\%) and Tier~2 (loop compression; 77--86\%) are separated by 12+pp with non-overlapping confidence intervals.
27	These results demonstrate that for SFT-based verdict prediction, more trace information impairs learning efficiency; the binding constraint is information density, not quantity.
28	\end{abstract}
29	
30	\input{introduction}
31	\input{method}
32	\input{experiments}
33	\input{discussion}
34	\input{related_work}
35	\input{conclusion}
36	
37	\section*{Limitations}
38	
39	Our study has several limitations.
40	First, all experiments use HumanEval and MBPP-sanitized (591 problems, simple assert-based tests on short Python functions); real-world failure modes including integration tests, parametric tests, and flaky tests remain untested, and generalization to human-written code, other languages, and multi-file projects is unverified.
41	Second, all four models (1.5B--13B) are code-specialized; whether these findings extend to general-purpose LLMs or larger scales is untested.
42	Third, our analysis is limited to verdict prediction; whether the two-pathway model transfers to other trace-based tasks (e.g., output prediction, program repair) is an open question.
43	
44	\section*{Ethics Statement}
45	
46	This work uses publicly available benchmarks (HumanEval, MBPP-sanitized, DebugBench) and does not involve human subjects, crowdsourced annotations, or personally identifiable information.
47	All models evaluated are open-source and publicly accessible.
48	Training uses parameter-efficient LoRA fine-tuning on enterprise-grade GPUs, with each experiment using a single GPU.
49	Total compute is approximately 320 GPU-hours across all conditions, models, seeds, and iterative refinements (including approximately 170 hours for successful experiments; individual runs range from 0.5h for small models to 2.2h for CodeLlama-13B, with the total reflecting 4 models $\times$ multiple conditions $\times$ multiple seeds $\times$ iterative development).
50	We do not foresee direct negative societal impacts from this work; verdict prediction is a developer-facing tool for automated testing.
```

Now I'm realizing the actual mechanism: tail conditions have zero diagnostic tokens because FullTraceFormatter only pulls from the line and locals fields, never from exception data. The performance jump isn't from capturing exception tracebacks at all—it's from crossing the threshold where the globals dump starts including `[Line X]` structural markers.

Looking at the C2 and C3 sections, C2 is resolved with the abstract correction, but C3 still has mismatched numbers in the appendix table and text that need updating.

The discrepancy hinges on which DeepSeek value they used—if they applied the corrected 80.7% figure, the minimum gap would be 13.2pp, but the caption shows 13.7pp, suggesting they either excluded DeepSeek entirely or relied on the incorrect 82.9% value, making CodeLlama's 13.7pp the minimum instead. Either way, there's an inconsistency between what the table displays and what the registry shows.

The bigger issue is in the appendix explanation about phase transitions—it claims exception tracebacks trigger the threshold shift, but that's factually incorrect since the tail conditions never include exception tracebacks at all. The actual mechanism is the globals module dump threshold, which the abstract now correctly identifies. So while the main sections have been fixed, the experiments.tex file still contains the original error. {"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"149-The remaining ${\\\\sim}$74\\\\% (+13.11pp) reflects information overload at inference: the model receives thousands of irrelevant trace lines that compete for attention with the diagnostic signal, regardless of loss design.\\n150-This asymmetry suggests that content selection may matter more than loss engineering.\\n151:Cross-model evidence supports this: the tail-truncation phase transition at ${\\\\sim}$500 tokens (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:trace_length}, all four models) coincides with exception traceback length, indicating that retaining diagnostic content is the critical factor.\\n152-Cross-scale validation on Qwen-1.5B yields a consistent pattern (17\\\\% gradient dilution, 83\\\\% information overload, 3 seeds), confirming that information overload dominates across model scales.\\n153-CodeLlama-13B and DeepSeek-6.7B both exhibit label-only collapse (28.5\\\\% accuracy, collapsing to the minority class), limiting the decomposition to models whose tokenizers preserve sufficient trace structure under the training sequence length.\\n--\\n169-A cross-model budget sweep across four models and three architecture families reveals a phase transition localized between 384 and 512 tail tokens.\\n170-At tail-384, all models remain near baseline (73.6--82.9\\\\%, only +1.0--6.5pp over tail-256); the steep jump occurs at 384$\\\\to$512 (+11.0--22.0pp), crossing from near-baseline to near-ceiling (${\\\\sim}$94--98\\\\%).\\n171:This localizes the critical threshold to ${\\\\sim}$400--500 tokens, matching the typical exception traceback length (Table~\\\\ref{tab:tail_cross_model}).\\n172-Beyond 512 tokens, accuracy converges further: multi-seed validation across all 12 model$\\\\times$seed combinations confirms all tail-512 accuracies fall within a 4.63pp band [93.61\\\\%, 98.24\\\\%] (Table~\\\\ref{tab:tail512_seeds}); at tail-1024, three models reach 96.5--98.2\\\\%, compressing the 6.3pp cross-model spread observed at tail-256.\\n173-\\\\texttt{tail\\\\_2048} captures 95--98\\\\% of \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only}'s accuracy across all completed configurations.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.44}}"}
```

{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"116-\\\\STATE \\\\textbf{// Component 1: Exception summary}\\n117-\\\\FOR{each event $e$ in $T$ with type \\\\texttt{exception}}\\n118:  \\\\STATE Parse traceback $\\\\rightarrow$ extract error type, message, file, line\\n119-  \\\\STATE Append \\\\texttt{[ERROR] type: message (file:line)} to $V$\\n120-\\\\ENDFOR\\n121-\\\\IF{no exception events found}\\n--\\n358-\\\\centering\\n359-\\\\includegraphics[width=\\\\columnwidth]{budget_curve}\\n360:\\\\caption{Cross-model tail-truncation budget curve ($N{=}1{,}190$, four models, three architecture families). All models remain near baseline at tail-256/384, then jump +13.7--22.0pp at 384$\\\\to$512, converging into a 93.6--98.2\\\\% band by tail-1024. The shaded region marks the transition zone where the exception traceback (${\\\\sim}$400--500 tokens) is captured. Dashed line: \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} range (99.3--99.9\\\\%); dotted line: \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} range (67--77\\\\%).}\\n361-\\\\label{fig:trace_length}\\n362-\\\\end{figure}\\n363-\\n--\\n420-However, \\\\texttt{tail\\\\_256} (77.48\\\\%) performs at the \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} baseline level (75.13\\\\%), indicating that the diagnostic signal does not reside in the very last trace lines.\\n421-The critical jump occurs between 256 and 512 tail tokens (+16.13pp), localizing the signal window to approximately 256--512 tokens from the trace terminus.\\n422:This is consistent with Python's execution model: exception tracebacks and assertion comparisons appear near the end of the trace but are preceded by the final function call frames, placing them typically 300--500 tokens from the terminus rather than at the absolute end.\\n423-\\n424-The 18.07pp gap from \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace} to \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} decomposes as: 86\\\\% positional concentration (recovered by tail-truncation at 2048 tokens) and 14\\\\% residual content noise (the 2.52pp gap from \\\\texttt{tail\\\\_2048} to \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} that persists regardless of truncation direction).\\n425-The fact that \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} (99.83\\\\%) still outperforms \\\\texttt{tail\\\\_2048} (97.31\\\\%) confirms that semantic extraction (identifying the exception type and message) is superior to positional extraction (keeping a fixed window), because the model need not filter out non-diagnostic lines that happen to fall within the window.\\n--\\n470-At tail-384, all four models remain near baseline (73.6--82.9\\\\%), gaining only +1.0--6.5pp over tail-256.\\n471-The steep jump occurs at 384$\\\\to$512: +13.7pp (Qwen-7B), +11.0pp (DeepSeek), +17.6pp (CodeLlama), +22.0pp (Qwen-1.5B), crossing from near-baseline to near-ceiling.\\n472:This localizes the critical threshold to ${\\\\sim}$400--500 tokens, consistent with the typical exception traceback length: below ${\\\\sim}$384 tokens, the traceback is truncated and no diagnostic event is captured; above ${\\\\sim}$512, it is fully included regardless of model capacity or architecture.\\n473-\\n474-\\\\emph{Convergence at tail-512+}: once the phase transition is crossed, accuracy converges across all models into a 93.6--98.2\\\\% band at tail-512 (seed 42).\\n475-Multi-seed validation confirms robustness: across all 12 model$\\\\times$seed combinations at tail-512 (Table~\\\\ref{tab:tail512_seeds}), all accuracies fall within [93.61\\\\%, 98.24\\\\%], a 4.63pp band that compresses the signal-poor tail-256 spread (6.3pp) by 27\\\\%.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.53}}"}

Now I have a complete picture of remaining issues. Let me categorize what was fixed and what wasn't:

**experiments.tex:**
1. Line 151: "coincides with exception traceback length" ← WRONG
2. Line 170: "73.6--82.9%" ← Also C3 related, uses wrong DeepSeek 82.9%
3. Line 170: "+11.0--22.0pp" ← Wrong because of wrong DeepSeek data (C3)


4. Line 171: "matching the typical exception traceback length" ← WRONG

**appendix.tex:**
5. Line 118 in the algorithm pseudocode is actually correct—it properly describes how exception_only parses actual tracebacks rather than the tail condition.
6. Line 360 in the figure caption incorrectly references exception traceback length for tail conditions.
7. Line 422 mentions exception tracebacks and assertion comparisons appearing near the end of the trace.
8. Line 471: The DeepSeek gap calculation is wrong; it should be 13.2pp not 11.0pp.
9. Line 472 incorrectly explains the truncation threshold, claiming tracebacks below ~384 tokens are truncated and above ~512 are fully included.

**C2 (72-82%) — FIXED:**
- Abstract updated to "77--82%" ✅
- Conclusion rewritten without specific range ✅
- But experiments.tex line 170 still contains "73.6--82.9%" at tail, which needs fixing.

**C3 (DeepSeek tail-384 = 82.9%) — NOT FIXED:**
The table and multiple text locations still show the incorrect 82.9% value for DeepSeek tail-384. There's also an inconsistency between the figure caption showing "+13.7--22.0pp" and line 170 showing "+11.0--22.0pp", suggesting the caption may have been partially corrected but the rest of the document wasn't updated consistently. I'm noticing the abstract correctly identifies the mechanism as crossing the globals module dump threshold, but experiments.tex and appendix.tex contradict this by attributing the jump to exception traceback length instead. This is a significant narrative inconsistency that needs to be resolved across the paper.

Looking at the file modification timestamps, it appears the abstract and introduction were updated on May 22, but experiments.tex and appendix.tex weren't touched since May 21, which means those sections still contain the outdated explanation that conflicts with the corrected abstract. Looking at the specific issues that remain — C1 appears in five more locations across experiments and appendix, C3 wasn't addressed at all with the DeepSeek metric still showing the wrong percentage, and there's now a caption-to-text mismatch on the performance gains. I'm torn on whether to flag this immediately or wait for the Paper Agent to request review, since my instructions suggest they should initiate the review cycle rather than me proactively catching errors.

But my mandate is clear: when I spot writing inconsistencies that stem from incomplete revisions, I should report them to the Director. The cross-dataset results are actually ready to incorporate now, which could strengthen the paper's claims, so I should document that the HE→MBPP verification is still running while the reverse direction is complete.

{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"403:Under tail-truncation, accuracy increases monotonically for Qwen-7B and Qwen-1.5B (Table~\\\\ref{tab:tail_cross_model}); only DeepSeek-6.7B shows a mild $-$1.5pp decrease at tail-1024$\\\\to$tail-2048, attributable to architecture-specific long-context sensitivity rather than a systematic mid-length pathology.\\n404-\\n405-\\\\paragraph{Comparison to baselines.}\\n406-All four trace budget conditions significantly exceed \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} except 1024 tokens ($p{=}.078$), confirming that raw trace content provides some signal but unreliably so.\\n407-The gap to \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} (99.83\\\\%) is massive for every budget condition (18--23pp, all $p{<}.001$), underscoring that even unlimited raw traces cannot substitute for targeted signal extraction.\\n408-The best full-trace condition (no\\\\_limit, 81.76\\\\%) still lags \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} by 18.07pp (95\\\\% CI [$-$20.34, $-$15.88]).\\n409-\\n410-\\\\paragraph{Interpretation.}\\n411-These results rule out input truncation as the primary explanation for the full-trace performance gap: even with unlimited tokens, the model cannot extract the diagnostic signal that VOTA's targeted compression provides.\\n412-The non-monotonic dip at 1024 tokens is consistent with a ``worst-of-both-worlds'' regime where the model receives enough noisy context to dilute the signal but not enough to compensate through broader coverage.\\n413-The result reinforces the information density interpretation (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:density}): the gap between full traces and VOTA is driven by the \\\\emph{content} of the trace (irrelevant lines diluting the diagnostic signal), not its \\\\emph{length}.\\n414-In contrast, VOTA's compressed representation concentrates the diagnostic signal into fewer tokens, making it sensitive to truncation when those tokens are removed (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:cross_model}).\\n415-\\n416-\\\\paragraph{Truncation direction.}\\n417-Tail-truncation (retaining the last $k$ tokens) dramatically outperforms head-truncation: \\\\texttt{tail\\\\_2048} achieves 97.31\\\\% vs.\\\\ \\\\texttt{head\\\\_2048} (no\\\\_limit) at 81.76\\\\%, a 15.55pp improvement.\\n418-Even \\\\texttt{tail\\\\_512} (93.61\\\\%) exceeds every head-truncation condition by over 11pp.\\n--\\n428:Table~\\\\ref{tab:tail_cross_model} extends the tail-truncation analysis to a full budget sweep across four models spanning three architecture families and an 8.7$\\\\times$ parameter range.\\n429-\\n430-\\\\begin{table}[t]\\n431-\\\\centering\\n432-\\\\caption{Cross-model tail-truncation budget curve ($N{=}1{,}190$). All four models exhibit a transition between tail-384 and tail-512 (+13--23pp). $\\\\downarrow$: non-monotonic decrease. $^\\\\dag$CodeLlama tail-1024 trained with label-only loss to address tokenizer-driven gradient dilution (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}). $^\\\\ddag$CodeLlama tail-2048 outlier (41.8\\\\%): LoRA long-sequence instability under label-only loss; excluded from cross-model analysis.}\\n433:\\\\label{tab:tail_cross_model}\\n434-\\\\small\\n435-\\\\resizebox{\\\\columnwidth}{!}{%\\n436-\\\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\\n437-\\\\toprule\\n438-\\\\textbf{Model} & \\\\textbf{tail-256} & \\\\textbf{tail-384} & \\\\textbf{tail-512} & \\\\textbf{tail-1024} & \\\\textbf{tail-2048} & \\\\textbf{exc\\\\_only} \\\\\\\\\\n439-\\\\midrule\\n440-Qwen-7B (7B) & 78.9 & 79.9 & 93.6 & 96.5 & 97.3 & 99.83 \\\\\\\\\\n441-DeepSeek (6.7B) & 76.4 & 82.9 & 93.9 & 96.5 & 95.0$\\\\downarrow$ & 99.33 \\\\\\\\\\n442-CodeLlama (13B) & 77.9 & 80.6 & 98.2 & 98.2$^\\\\dag$ & 41.8$^\\\\ddag$ & 99.89 \\\\\\\\\\n443-Qwen-1.5B (1.5B) & 72.6 & 73.6 & 95.6 & 96.6 & 97.9 & 99.83 \\\\\\\\\\n444-\\\\bottomrule\\n445-\\\\end{tabular}%\\n446-}\\n447-\\\\end{table}\\n448-\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.44}}"}

| CodeLlama | 80.6 | 98.2 | +17.6pp |


| Qwen-1.5B | 73.6 | 95.6 | +22.0pp |

The range across all models spans from +11.0pp to +22.0pp, but there's an inconsistency in how the paper reports this. The appendix figure caption restricts it to +13.7–22.0pp, excluding DeepSeek's lower value, while the main text includes the full +11.0–22.0pp range. The appendix text lists all four models individually, so there's a discrepancy in what gets highlighted where.

Looking at what happens if DeepSeek's value gets corrected to 80.7%, the gap would shift to 13.2pp, making it the new minimum instead of Qwen-7B's 13.7pp. That would adjust the overall range to +13.2–22.0pp, which still doesn't match the appendix caption's +13.7–22.0pp claim.

I'm also noticing the table caption at line 432 states "+13–23pp," but the actual maximum gap is 22.0pp from Qwen-1.5B, not 23pp—so that's another discrepancy to track down. Let me double-check the DeepSeek tail-384 registry value to make sure I have the right baseline.

Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. The correlation checks out at r=0.949, confirming the diagnostic token ratio finding. The worker's analysis validates my critical finding that tail conditions have zero diagnostic tokens, which directly refutes the exception traceback explanation. <details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
This is excellent data. Let me analyze:

**Clustered Bootstrap Results:**
- 10,000 bootstrap resamples, problem-level clustering (119 problems, 10 samples each)
- Key finding: CIs are ~2.5-2.7× wider than sample-level CIs for the conditions with lower accuracy
- `exception_only` vs `loop_only`: significant (p≈0.0), diff = 14.2pp, CI [9.2, 19.6]
- `exception_only` vs `ae_only`: NOT significant (p=0.47), diff = -0.08pp


- `loop_only` vs `full_trace`: NOT significant (p=0.06), diff = 3.9pp — this is interesting because under clustered bootstrap they don't differ significantly
- `exception_only` vs `no_trace`: significant (p≈0.0), diff = 24.7pp
- The two-tier structure holds up: Tier 1 (exception_only/ae_only around 99.8-99.9%) sits ~14pp above Tier 2 (loop_only at 85.6%), and this gap is significant

Now I'm looking at the diagnostic token ratio across all 15 conditions to see how it correlates with accuracy. Now I'm seeing the complete performance metrics across all configurations. The critical insight is that the tail-based approaches—whether capturing 256, 384, 512, 1024, or 2048 tokens—all show essentially zero diagnostic ratio yet still achieve accuracy ranging from 0.80 to 0.97. This reveals an implicit pathway where the model can solve the task without explicit diagnostic tokens. The correlation coefficient drops from 0.949 with just five conditions to 0.629 with all fifteen because these tail conditions introduce substantial variance—near-zero diagnostic contribution but widely divergent accuracy scores—which is exactly what I'd expect given this hidden mechanism.

The `nomarker` configuration is particularly telling: it strips away the explicit diagnostic marker entirely, yielding zero diagnostic ratio, yet still reaches 0.9782 accuracy. This suggests the model is learning from structural patterns in the trace itself—likely the class balance and pass/fail distribution—rather than relying on explicit diagnostic signals. Similarly, `full_trace` shows only 0.294 diagnostic tokens on average, which makes sense now that I understand the "[No trace available]" markers are being counted as diagnostic; that tiny value comes from occasional markers in pass samples.

Looking at the worker's analysis, the statistical work checks out: the budget curve confirms zero tracebacks across all tail conditions, the correlation coefficients are properly computed (r=0.949 for N=5, r=0.629 for the extended set), and the pairwise comparisons correctly identify significance between Tier 1 and Tier 2 but not between Tier 2 and full_trace. However, there's a critical issue: the paper's explanation about exception traceback length is factually contradicted by this budget curve analysis, and the extended correlation of r=0.629 represents a meaningful caveat that should be surfaced in the paper alongside the headline r=0.949 figure.

The `nomarker` condition is particularly revealing—it achieves 97.82% accuracy with zero diagnostic ratio, which breaks the correlation and suggests the model is leveraging non-diagnostic features like trace length or trace presence to succeed even without explicit markers, pointing to an implicit pathway the paper hasn't fully accounted for. I should check the pass_fail_diff directory next to complete the picture.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
This is very comprehensive analysis. Let me summarize the key findings from the worker:

**1. Budget Curve Diagnostic Analysis:**
- ALL tail conditions: `has_full_traceback_count = 0` — zero tracebacks
- full_trace also has `has_full_traceback_count = 0` — confirming FullTraceFormatter discards exception info
- Worker verdict: "NOT SUPPORTED - full_trace format has no diagnostic markers"

**2. Diagnostic Token Ratio:**
- Main 5 conditions: r=0.949, p=0.014 ✅ (matches paper)


- When I expand to 15 conditions, the correlation weakens to r=0.629 but stays significant at p=0.012, while all tail conditions cluster near zero diagnostic ratio—the tiny 0.001 values come from counting "[No trace available]" markers for those 70 silent failures.

**3. Clustered Bootstrap (N=10000, 119 problems):**
- The exception_only versus loop_only comparison shows a clear significant difference with p≈0 and a 14.2 percentage point gap, confirming the Tier 1 versus Tier 2 distinction, but loop_only versus full_trace doesn't reach significance at p=0.06.
- exception_only and ae_only both show no significant difference, placing them in the same tier, though the confidence intervals are roughly 2.5 times wider than what I'd see at the sample level.

**4. Pass/Fail Diff Analysis:**
- In full_trace, 89.6% of the token difference comes from the trace itself while only 10.4% is in code changes and none in tests—the trace breakdown shows structural markers dominate at 42 tokens and runtime context at 14703, with zero exception diagnostics.
- exception_only achieves a diagnostic ratio of 1.0, meaning 100% of its trace difference is diagnostic content, and as a verdict predictor it covers 100% of cases with 99.33% accuracy, whereas full_trace only covers 5.9% through those "[No trace available]" markers.

**Key finding for the paper:**

The 8 "pass" samples flagged with error markers in exception_only represent caught exceptions, and the verdict predictor's 99.33% accuracy (1182/1190) matches the B5 regex baseline exactly—those 8 misclassifications are false positives from caught exceptions.

**Now for my review assessment:**

The worker's quantitative analysis solidly supports the two-pathway model, but the paper still contains the factually incorrect traceback explanation in the experiments and appendix sections, and the DeepSeek tail-384 showing 82.9% error remains unresolved. I need to compile my findings for the Director, acknowledging the solid analysis while emphasizing that the critical issues from the previous round haven't been fully addressed in the paper.

There's also an internal inconsistency—the abstract states the correct explanation while the experiments and appendix contain the wrong one. I should verify the cross-dataset HE→MBPP results look sound, and I'm noting that the nomarker condition (97.82% accuracy) demonstrates the model learns from exception content itself rather than just the marker pattern, since the B5 regex drops dramatically to 28.49% without the marker while the SFT model maintains strong performance.
```

The reframe is strategically sound. After 15 rounds of defending the Two-Pathway Model, shifting the narrative from "exception events work" to "complete traces actively degrade" transforms the finding from something that feels tautological into a genuinely counterintuitive result with real practical implications.

Now I'm checking whether the data actually supports this stronger claim. The numbers show full_trace performance at 77-82% across models, exception_only consistently above 99%, and no_trace around 72-77%—so the gap between full_trace and exception_only is substantial at 18+ percentage points, while full_trace barely edges out no_trace in some cases. That's the surprising pattern worth highlighting. The question is whether "complete traces degrade verdict prediction" is a contribution strong enough for EMNLP, and I think it is if positioned as counterintuitive and systematic across multiple model families.

The risk is that a reviewer dismisses this as just another case of LLMs struggling with long inputs, which is already known. So the paper needs to clearly distinguish this from generic length degradation by emphasizing that it's about diagnostic signal dilution specifically. For the main framing, elevating B5 regex as the primary baseline makes sense—it's currently undersold, but showing it achieves 99.33% while being fragile without markers (dropping to 28.49%), then demonstrating that SFT provides calibrated probabilities and cross-dataset generalization, creates a much stronger narrative arc.

I'm noticing a discrepancy in the numbers though—the plan mentions "nomarker → ~91%" but the registry shows nomarker SFT at 97.82%, and the B5 regex on nomarker data is what drops to 28.49%. The 91% might be referencing an earlier seed or different condition, but the core insight remains: the gap between SFT and regex on nomarker data is substantial, which is the key evidence for why the marker matters.

The reframing from "exceptions help" to "complete traces hurt" is cleaner, though I should be precise about what the data actually shows: complete traces with noise degrade performance compared to targeted extraction, there's a clear tier hierarchy in the data, and the degradation stems from content rather than just token count. On the MF-2 assessment, the claim about "~74% of the gap attributable to information overload" is definitely overclaimed—the label-only loss experiment conflates multiple factors like gradient dilution, effective learning rate changes, and optimization dynamics, so isolating a single cause is problematic.

I think keeping "two-pathway model" as a descriptive framework is reasonable, as long as I'm honest that it's an organizing principle rather than a proven causal mechanism. The issue wasn't the terminology itself but claiming causal decomposition when the evidence doesn't support it. For MF-3, the reviewer has a point about multiple comparison correction—with roughly 15 pairwise comparisons, I need to apply something like Holm-Bonferroni or Benjamini-Hochberg depending on whether I'm controlling family-wise error or false discovery rate.

Since the paper makes specific claims about large effects like the Tier 1 vs Tier 2 gap and exception_only vs full_trace differences, Holm-Bonferroni is the safer choice to ensure confidence in each individual claim. The borderline cases will likely drop out under correction anyway, while the large effects should hold up. I'll use Holm-Bonferroni for the main results but also report the FDR-corrected values for completeness.

On the bootstrap issue with duplicates—the dataset structure means standard resampling underestimates variance since problems repeat across samples. The clustered bootstrap approach, resampling at the problem level rather than the sample level, correctly preserves the correlation structure within problems. With effects in the 14-28pp range, this wider confidence interval from the clustered approach is the right way to assess uncertainty.

Looking at the actual clustered bootstrap results, the main comparisons hold up: exception_only beats loop_only by 14.2pp and no_trace by 24.7pp, both significant. The loop_only versus full_trace gap of 3.9pp is borderline at p=0.06, but that's a secondary claim anyway.

For the Information Bottleneck framing, the idea is that exception_only functions as a bottleneck that filters out irrelevant information.

The LIMA connection about "less is more" in supervised fine-tuning directly supports this and strengthens the theoretical foundation. I should replace "information overload" with "diagnostic signal dilution" since it's more technically precise. The cross-dataset results showing 99.41% versus 70.56% on HE→MBPP are compelling evidence that exception events transfer across tasks rather than being format-specific, so those need to go in. The training dynamics figure would help illustrate the convergence versus oscillation pattern. Three seeds is a fair limitation given how large the main effects are. The k-means clustering for the two-tier approach is intriguing but risky if the gap statistic doesn't actually support k=2.

The 14 percentage point gap with non-overlapping confidence intervals is strong enough on its own without needing formal clustering validation. Rolling variance or coefficient of variation would quantify the oscillation pattern nicely as supplementary evidence. Releasing code and data is standard practice. Looking at the overall assessment, the reframing helps but probably won't push this to 3.5+ in three days because the core issue—that the finding borders on tautological—hasn't been fully resolved. Even reframing it as "complete traces hurt" invites the obvious objection that long noisy inputs naturally degrade LLM performance. The paper's real strength lies in the magnitude of degradation at 18+ percentage points, which is genuinely surprising, combined with the mechanism analysis distinguishing between gradient dilution and inference-time overload.

Cross-dataset transfer hitting 99.41% demonstrates practical value, and the phase transition at tail-512 reveals non-obvious structure that goes beyond what you'd expect. I shouldn't delete the Two-Pathway Model entirely—just reposition it from a core contribution to a descriptive framework. The practical recommendation about prioritizing exception events first with tail truncation as a fallback should take more prominence. For the three-day deadline, the computational items are all CPU-bound and fast, so the real bottleneck is rewriting the framing, which is doable in a day. Cross-dataset training should wrap up today. This timeline is actually feasible. The no-marker experiment is critical for defending against the tautology charge in MF-1.

The nomarker collapse to 28.49% for B5 regex while SFT holds at 97.82% is the strongest evidence that SFT captures genuine patterns rather than just format artifacts—this needs to move from the appendix into the main narrative. Cross-dataset transfer at 99.41% for SFT versus 70.56% without traces shows the exception-only approach learns reasoning that generalizes, which is new evidence from this round. The traceback language inconsistencies I flagged earlier become even more critical now—if the paper pivots toward "complete traces are harmful" and mechanism analysis, having incorrect mechanism descriptions scattered through experiments and appendix undermines the entire reframing. That has to be fixed as part of the MF-1 and MF-2 revisions.

The DeepSeek tail-384 discrepancy between 82.9% and 80.7% needs correction, especially since the phase transition now serves as supporting evidence for the reframe and precision matters.

This operationalizes \\\"information density\\\" without circularity — the metric is computed from data alone, independent of model accuracy.\\n\\n**Pathway 2 — Implicit terminal state signals**: Tail-truncated traces (last N tokens of full input) achieve 93-97% accuracy with near-zero explicit diagnostic tokens ([ERROR]=0 in all tail conditions). ECE=0.0027\\n- `deepseek_exception_only_seed42`: DeepSeek 99.83% (epoch1) / 99.33% (epoch3)\\n- `deepseek_exception_only_seed456`: DeepSeek 99.33% (epoch3), CI [98.82, 99.75] — 与 seed42 完全一致\\n- `deepseek_exception_only_seed123`: DeepSeek 91.43% (epoch1) / 91.09% (epoch3) — LoRA init sensitivity outlier\\n- `deepseek_vota_seed42`: DeepSeek VOTA 84.45% (gap = 14.96pp vs Qwen)\\n- `deepseek_no_trace_seed42`: DeepSeek no_trace 73.36% (gap = 1.77pp vs Qwen)\\n- `vota_no_ae_seed42`: 99.41% — actual/expected removal 不降，非 trivial string comparison\\n\\n**DeepSeek seed analysis** ✅ (FINALIZED, D029):\\n\\n| Seed | Epoch 3 (final) | fail_recall | pass_recall | train_loss | Errors | CI |\\n|------|-----------------|-------------|-------------|------------|--------|-----|\\n| 42   | 99.33%          | 1.0         | 0.9906      | —          | 8/1190 | — |\\n| 456  | 99.33%          | 1.0         | 0.9906      | 0.1225     | 8/1190 | [98.82, 99.75] |\\n| 123  | 91.09%          | 0.705       | 0.993       | 0.122      | —      | [89.4, 92.6] |\\n\\nseed42 与 seed456 结果**完全一致**（均 8/1190 错误，99.33%），seed123 为离群值。seed123 的 fail_recall 仅 0.705 — LoRA 初始化落入差的局部最优，未充分学到 exception→fail 映射。**关键发现**: seed456 和 seed123 的 train_loss 几乎相同（0.1225 vs 0.122），但 accuracy 差 8.2pp — token-level loss 掩盖了 sample-level 分类能力的巨大差异（详见 D10）。\\n\\n**Irreducible error analysis** ✅: 3 seeds 错误的 2 个样本 (id=901, 905) 均来自 HumanEval/95 (`check_dict_case`)，根因为 Python `all()` 对 generator expression 短路触发 `GeneratorExit`（`BaseException` 子类，非真正错误）。tracer 将其标记为 `[ERROR]`，exception_only 模型看到 7 行 `[ERROR] GeneratorExit`，合理但错误地预测 fail。整个 1190 条测试集仅这 2 条含 `GeneratorExit`，模型无训练信号学习其良性本质。**这确认 99.83% 是 exception_only 表征的理论上限，剩余 0.17% 是 representation-inherent irreducible error，而非模型不足。**\\n\\n**Seed robustness** ✅: Qwen exception_only 3-seed 完全一致，构成本文最强 robustness 证据：\\n\\n| Seed | Accuracy | 95% CI | Errors |\\n|------|----------|--------|--------|\\n| 42 | 99.83% | [99.58, 100] | 2/1190 |\\n| 123 | 99.83% | [99.58, 100] | 2/1190 |\\n| 456 | 99.83% | [99.58, 100] | 2/1190 |\\n\\n3 seeds 错误样本 100% 重叠（id=901, 905, 均为 `check_dict_case` 函数, label=pass → pred=fail）。**Irreducible samples**: 模型已达数据集理论上限，variance = 0。\\n\\n**DeepSeek cross-model** ✅ (D029): 3-seed 完整数据 — seed42/456=99.33%, seed123=91.09%。2/3 seeds 鲁棒，seed123 为 LoRA 初始化离群值（fail_recall 仅 0.705）。Qwen 3-seed 完美稳定 (99.83% ± 0.00) vs DeepSeek 高方差（σ≈4.8pp），暗示 exception signal 的 learnability 与模型 LoRA 训练稳定性相关。但即使最差 seed (91.09%) 仍优于 VOTA (84.45%) 和 no_trace (73.36%)，relative dominance robust。\\n\\n### Claim 2 [UPGRADED v5.0]: Two-Pathway Model — diagnostic token ratio operationalizes information density (non-circular)\\n\\n**The paper's core analytical contribution.** We operationalize \\\"information density\\\" with a model-free metric and discover it only captures one of two independent signal pathways:\\n\\n**Diagnostic token ratio** = (tokens matching exception/assertion patterns) / (total sequence tokens). Computed entirely from data, independent of model accuracy. For the 5 main conditions (no_trace, full_trace, exception_only, ae_only, loop_only): Pearson r=0.949, p=0.014. This is the **explicit pathway**.\\n\\n**Extended to 15 conditions** reveals the **implicit pathway**: tail-512 to tail-2048 achieve 93-97% accuracy with diagnostic ratio ≈ 0.001 (essentially zero). The correlation drops to r=0.630 (Spearman r=0.766) — because the implicit pathway operates on different signals entirely.\\n\\n**Three-pathway framework**:\\n1. **Explicit** (diagnostic tokens → 99%+): exception/assertion markers directly encode verdict. Captured by diagnostic token ratio.\\n2. **Implicit** (terminal execution state → 93-97%): `[Line X]` variable values at final execution steps encode program outcome indirectly. Accessible only when tail truncation crosses the globals dump noise floor (~384 tokens). FullTraceFormatter does NOT render exception info in tail data ([ERROR]=0%), so this is a genuinely independent signal channel (D115).\\n3. **Noise dilution** (intermediate states → 72-82%): Both signals exist in full traces but are buried under thousands of intermediate execution state tokens → training oscillation, overfitting to spurious patterns.\\n\\n**Phase transition evidence**: tail-384 (79.8%) → tail-512 (93.6%) = +13.8pp. Agent confirmed: tail-384 has 0 `[Line X]` events (1\\n\\n... <details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have enough context. Let me also check the clustered bootstrap data I already read to verify claims about pairwise significance.

From my earlier analysis:
- exception_only vs loop_only: gap=14.2pp, significant (p≈0)
- loop_only vs full_trace: gap=3.9pp, NOT significant (p=0.06)
- exception_only vs no_trace: gap=24.7pp, significant (p≈0)

Now let me compose my comprehensive evaluation. I need to assess each item.

Key considerations for my evaluation:


The near-tautological framing is the core problem that's persisted through 15 rounds—the Director's reframe helps, but I need to determine if it actually resolves the fundamental issue. The "complete traces hurt" finding is genuinely surprising and could be the paper's real contribution, though a reviewer will likely dismiss it as generic long-context degradation rather than something specific to diagnostic signal dilution. I also need to be honest about B5 regex as the primary baseline instead of burying it, which the current strategy seems to do. And I should evaluate the nomarker and cross-dataset results to see what they actually demonstrate.

The nomarker results are compelling—B5 drops to 28.49% while SFT holds at 97.82%, and the cross-dataset transfer at 99.41% suggests SFT learns something genuinely transferable rather than memorizing. These results are my strongest defense against the tautology charge and deserve prominence. The DDL timeline is tight but feasible at 3 days if I focus the rewrite effort, though I still need to fix the "traceback" language in the appendix and correct the DeepSeek 82.9% error that's been flagged multiple times.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have all the data I need to write a comprehensive evaluation. Let me analyze each item:

**Clustered Bootstrap Data (119 problems, N=10000):**

Pairwise tests (all already using problem-level clustering):
- exception_only vs loop_only: diff=14.2pp, CI [9.2, 19.6], p≈0 ✅ significant
- exception_only vs full_trace: diff=18.1pp, CI [12.4, 24.1], p≈0 ✅ significant


- exception_only vs no_trace: diff=24.7pp, CI [18.2, 31.6], p≈0 ✅ significant
- ae_only vs loop_only: diff=14.3pp, CI [9.3, 19.7], p≈0 ✅ significant
- vota vs full_trace: diff=17.6pp, CI [12.2, 23.5], p≈0 ✅ significant
- loop_only vs full_trace: diff=3.9pp, CI [-1.1, 8.9], p=0.06 ❌ NOT significant
- exception_only vs ae_only: diff=-0.08pp, CI [-0.5, 0.3], p=0.47 ❌ NOT significant (both Tier 1)
- vota_no_ae vs vota: diff=0.0pp, p=0.65 ❌ NOT significant

The core findings hold up under clustered bootstrap — Tier 1 methods substantially outperform Tier 2 and full traces, and complete traces are indeed harmful. The one limitation is that loop_only doesn't significantly beat full_trace when accounting for clustering, so I can't make that specific claim. Looking at the confidence interval width ratios, exception_only and ae_only show much tighter intervals relative to the sample-based ones, while the broader methods like no_trace and full_trace have wider clustered intervals.

Now I'm thinking through the multiple comparison problem. With 9 pairwise tests in the bootstrap data, I need to identify which comparisons are actually critical to the paper's main claims and apply a correction like Holm-Bonferroni accordingly.

Looking at the p-values when I sort them and apply Holm-Bonferroni sequentially—comparing each p-value to progressively less stringent thresholds—the six tests with p≈0 easily pass even the strictest cutoffs. The borderline case is loop_only versus full_trace at p=0.06, which fails the correction when compared to α/3=0.0167, while exception_only versus ae_only at p=0.47 clearly doesn't survive the correction either.

The key insight is that the core claims remain robust because the effects are so pronounced that multiple comparison correction doesn't change the conclusions. Benjamini-Hochberg gives the same result, so MF-3 doesn't require substantial revision.

However, I need to flag three critical issues from my initial review that weren't addressed: the traceback language problem is only partially fixed (abstract is corrected but experiments and appendix still contain errors), the 72→77% claim wasn't fully resolved, and the DeepSeek score discrepancy remains unfixed. These factual errors need to be corrected as part of the MF-1/MF-2 revisions to maintain credibility.
```
